# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-11-05

## LLM Agents
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[A Framework for Context Enrichment and Selective Robustness in LLM Social Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06914v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Researchers use large language models as agents in social simulations, where agents are prompted with observations and background information intended to shape their actions. Yet LLM agents have been shown to substantially change their behavior under meaning-preserving prompt variations, creating a validation problem for social simulation. One plausible hypothesis is that this brittleness reflects a lack of grounding and can be reduced by enriching the context provided to the agents. Whether richer context improves robustness remains unclear, in part because context richness and robustness are rarely defined in ways that support controlled comparisons. We address the first need with controlled context enrichment, a set of prompt substitution rules that systematically enriches distinct types of context: neutral padding, generic social background, descriptive decision grounding, and normative decision grounding. We address the second by introducing selective robustness as a validation criterion: insensitivity to meaning-preserving prompt variation combined with a directional response to decision-relevant changes in the social world. We demonstrate the framework in a Prisoner's Dilemma-like game, showing how it can be used to identify which forms of context enrichment produce more desirable and robust agent behavior. We find that these forms of enrichment produce distinct behavioral patterns that vary between the two models tested.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the SocialAgent Workshop @ NeurIPS 2026. 12 pages, 1 figure, 2 tables</p></details> |
| **[MCP-Eco: A Linked Dataset of MCP Servers, Consumers, and Development Activity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06897v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Model Context Protocol (MCP) enables LLM agents to access external tools and data. Studying its software ecosystem requires connecting server interfaces, the projects that configure these servers, and their development activity. However, this information is scattered across directories, package registries, configuration files, and repository histories. We present MCP-Eco, a relational dataset that integrates these sources and links consumer configuration declarations to candidate server projects. Its frozen release contains 16,880,899 records across 19 Parquet tables, covering 78,302 candidate server projects, 16,165 candidate consuming repositories, and 9,200 configuration declarations. Of these declarations, 5,975 resolve to 5,138 distinct repository-server pairs. The dataset also includes tool, resource, and prompt descriptions, declared dependencies, and associated development records, including pull requests, reviews, issues, and commits. Provenance fields distinguish directory-reported capabilities from those observed through live probing and document discovery sources and declaration-matching methods. These connections support research on MCP interface design, configuration practices, and repository maintenance, while enabling practitioners to inspect tool schemas and existing integration configurations. The dataset and accompanying analysis scripts are publicly available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/mcp-research-alliance/mcp-ecosystem Demo video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=N7TqnNneSfU</p></details> |  |
| **[SpecCompact: Accelerating LLM Agent Context Compaction with Slot-Parallel Semantic Speculative Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06876v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents use context compaction to manage growing interaction histories, but summarizing long histories with large models can introduce substantial delays. Compaction therefore requires balancing information retention against latency. We observe that structured summary slots can retain information when generated independently. Based on this observation, we present SpecCompact, a framework that accelerates compaction through slot-parallel semantic speculation. SpecCompact fine-tunes a small drafter and generates slots in parallel. The target model verifies each proposed line and repairs rejected content, while subsequent drafting uses the updated slot prefix. System optimizations further reduce repeated context processing and model calls. Across AppWorld and $τ^3$ benchmarks with two target models, SpecCompact achieves $2.04$--$5.63\times$ compaction speedups over Hermes-style summarization. Separate downstream evaluations reveal model- and task-dependent performance trade-offs. A user study within Hermes further shows an $8.68\times$ median compaction speedup.</p></details> |  |
| **[On Turn-Level Best-of-N Sampling for Scaling LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06868v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this work, we evaluate a simple inference-time scaling approach based on \textit{turn-level best-of-N sampling} (\method) for large language model (LLM) agents on complex, long-horizon tasks. We instantiate this using a \textit{generator-selector} framework with two LLMs; at each turn, a \emph{generator} model generates $N$ candidate actions and a \emph{selector} model selects one of them, which is then executed in the environment. This paper is closely related to the work of \cite{zainullina2025guided} who used critic models for guided search at inference time. Different from their work, we evaluate if off-the-shelf, open-weight LLMs can be effective at comparing different candidate actions directly. Experiments on long-horizon terminal tasks show that \method can lead to significant performance improvements, both in terms of accuracy (5--10\%) and consistency across repeated attempts. Analysis of agent trajectories reveal a shift in agent behaviour towards desirable patterns such as reduction in number of attempts to solve the task, improved task instruction following, debugging and testing.</p></details> |  |
| **[AutoEnergyDesign: An LLM-Driven Agent for Automatic Energy System Design Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35160v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The design optimization of building energy systems represents a complex, high-dimensional search problem involving discrete technology selections, continuous sizing parameters, and intricate economic trade-offs in which every candidate must be evaluated by a year-long simulation. This paper presents AutoEnergyDesign, in which a Large Language Model (LLM) agent reasons over past simulation results, proposes configuration changes and learns in context from each outcome, while a git-based mechanism reverts changes that do not improve the design. The agent couples a reasoning model with explicit memories, a constrained action interface, and an environment-grounded keep/revert feedback signal. The simulator covers eight technologies and computes the 25-year Net Present Cost (NPC). On a case study with measured building loads, the agent, instantiated with three Claude models (Haiku 5.5, Sonnet 5.5 and Opus 5.5; ten runs of 50 simulations each), is compared with random search, Tree-structured Parzen Estimator (TPE) Bayesian optimization and a (1+1) evolution strategy that shares the agent's acceptance rule (ten seeds of 300 evaluations). With all three models the agent reaches an NPC of 3.53MEUR, 1.20% and 0.23% below the best results of TPE and of the evolution strategy, and comes within 1% of it after a median of 18 to 27.5 simulations; among the baselines, only the evolution strategy does so, after a median of 192 evaluations. Every decision is recorded, making the search auditable.</p></details> |  |
| **[SecureGARD: Dynamic Ontology-Guided Governance for Secure Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06682v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using large language model (LLM) agents translate model outputs into consequential actions, turning indirect prompt injection into an execution-security problem. Existing defenses constrain tool calls, trajectories, or information flows, but often leave action semantics, state dependencies, and evidence relations implicit, limiting generalization across heterogeneous tools and complicating safe updates. We present \ours{}, a dynamic ontology-guided governance framework. \ours{} grounds heterogeneous calls in a shared semantic representation and combines an executable ontology with episode-level task state to derive deterministic ready, waiting, and forbidden decisions. A waiting decision identifies an unmet but recoverable obligation, enabling bounded replanning without expanding the agent's authorization boundary. \ours{} evolves its ontology offline from verified training executions. An offline LLM proposes instance-independent action--state axioms, which are accepted only if they address ontology errors exposed by training failures, preserve logical consistency and protected entailments, and pass held-out validation before the ontology is frozen. We evaluate \ours{} on AgentDojo, ASB, and AgentDyn under benign and indirect prompt-injection settings. \ours{} achieves a 0\% attack success rate (ASR) across all three benchmarks while improving task utility under attack compared with existing baselines. These results show that explicit semantic governance can strengthen execution security without indiscriminate blocking.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgentTrustCI: Build-Time Policy, Grounding, and Trajectory Tests for Java LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06674v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Infrastructure security does not establish whether an LLM agent selected an allowed tool, grounded its answer in tool output, or followed a required workflow. AgentTrustCI is a Java/JUnit 5 framework that expresses these application-layer expectations as policy, grounding, and trajectory checks over recorded traces. A deterministic replay implementation supports repeatable build-time tests; a judge interface provides an extension point for optional semantic evaluation. On 55 synthetic cases spanning seven enterprise domains, the full pipeline correctly classifies all 30 injected faults and 25 passing controls, including ten non-trivial controls, and misses none of 12 designated critical cases. Each single-layer configuration correctly classifies 35 cases because the corpus deliberately isolates ten faults per layer. A grounded answer obtained through a disallowed sequence illustrates the additional coverage of trajectory checks. Repeated measurements quantify validator execution separately from process startup and reporting. These results establish coverage of the authored regression suite, not production accuracy. We describe a production-trace validation protocol, optional LLM-judge integration, and maintenance of expectations as workflows evolve. The contribution is a practical pattern for expressing behavioral contracts within an existing Java release pipeline.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication in the 2026 International Conference on Computer and Applications (ICCA)</p></details> |
| **[Does Personality Persuade? Investigating Personality Trait Prompting and Persuasion in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06572v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can be prompted to express different personality profiles, but it remains unclear how reliably they do so or whether personality affects their persuasiveness. We investigate these questions in two evaluations. First, we assess Big Five personality prompting using the IPIP-NEO-120, examining reproducibility, trait controllability, cross-trait effects, and unconditioned model profiles. Second, we conduct a within-subjects user study (N=32) in which participants discuss moral dilemmas with LLM agents whose personalities are aligned, mismatched, neutral, or unconditioned. We find that prompted profiles can be reproducible and responsive to requested traits, although controllability varies across models and changes to one trait can affect others. Unconditioned models also exhibit systematic personality profiles rather than neutral baselines. In the user study, personality alignment did not significantly affect perceived persuasiveness, stance switching, or confidence changes. Participants switched their initial stance in only 8 of 128 conversations, while confidence changes were more common. Participants' Big Five traits were not significantly associated with susceptibility to persuasion, but higher self-reported AI persuasion literacy was associated with less frequent confidence changes. Exploratory analysis showed that logical appeals dominated agent messages. These findings distinguish technical control over prompted LLM personality from observable influence on users and motivate evaluating persuasive interactions beyond explicit changes in decisions.</p></details> |  |
| **[When Do Summaries Become Instructions? Unsupported Policy in LLM Agents' Self-Written Handoff Summaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06545v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-running LLM agents compact their context, replacing most history with a summary. We ask whether handoff summaries state permissions or requirements the record does not support, whether such sentences change what the next instance (the successor) does, and where they come from. Synthetic tool-use tasks carry a ground-truth permission ledger; same-snapshot replay reruns the successor with those whole sentences replaced by neutral filler. Two AI readers, reading only rule-flagged held-out summaries, confirmed unsupported restrictions in 1.2% and 2.4% of all readable summaries from two open-weight models, and unsupported authorizations in 0.1% and 1.3%. For restrictions, a pre-specified replay test was not significant (p = 0.58); a follow-up, planned after it, found a fragile effect (p = 0.027). Neutralizing false approvals cut unauthorized actions from 24 to 7 of 120 held-out runs (post hoc; later set: 17 to 11 of 48). Most development restrictions followed the agent's earlier reasoning that an action needs approval (post hoc). In pre-specified tests, replacing the system prompt's "Some actions need approval." with one saying where requirements come from cut unsupported restrictions from 5.0% to 1.8% of development summaries and, on held-out templates, from 1.8% to 0.3% (both p < 10^-9), while requests for approvals the ledger requires barely changed. Code and data: https://github.com/Chuw1/handoff-audit</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 3 figures, 15 tables. Code and data: https://github.com/Chuw1/handoff-audit</p></details> |
| **[Tools Need Not Speak in Text: Latent Context Compression for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06500v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents typically append raw tool outputs to their context. Over multi-step interactions, long and noisy observations incur substantial context overhead and dilute the agent's attention to task-relevant information. Existing token pruning and summarization methods remain constrained by discrete text representations, limiting the achievable compression ratios. In this work, we challenge the prevailing assumption that agents must consume tool observations as explicit text tokens. To this end, we propose Coca-Cola, a continuous latent context compression model for LLM agents. Coca-Cola compresses tool observations into latent tokens and embeds them into the input embedding space of the agent. We further introduce Action-Preserving Training, which aligns the agent's next-action distributions under full and compressed observations. This trains the compression model to learn action-sufficient latent tokens that retain decision-critical information. Experiments with web-search agents on four QA benchmarks and two model families show that Coca-Cola reduces the agent's token consumption by 70.01%-89.24% and end-to-end runtime by 24.45%-55.97%, while consistently improving task performance. These results suggest that Coca-Cola offers a promising approach for context compression in agents. Our code is available at https://github.com/THU-Agent/Coca-cola.</p></details> |  |
| **[What Does Multi-Turn Reinforcement Learning Really Improve in LLM Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06483v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While multi-turn agentic reinforcement learning (RL) excels on long-horizon tasks, its performance gains remain highly sensitive to interaction constraints. Evaluating across sampling breadth $K$ and interaction depth $T$ via $Pass@(K,T)$, we show that trajectory-level RL advantages depend on sufficient turn budgets, diminishing significantly when budgets tighten. Besides, agentic RL does not uniformly elevate single-step decision quality; rather, its strength concentrates in error recovery and long-horizon resilience. To mitigate the deployment risks caused by this budget dependence, we provide a preliminary discussion on the benefits and limitations of process supervision signals and introduce BudgetMix RL as an alternative that varies interaction budgets during training. Experiments demonstrate that BudgetMix RL achieves robust performance across diverse execution settings. Project Page: https://rethinking-agentic-rl.github.io/</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://rethinking-agentic-rl.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[LITMUS: Benchmarking Behavioral Jailbreaks of LLM Agents in Real OS Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10779v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid proliferation of LLM-based autonomous agents in real operating system environments introduces a new category of safety risk beyond content safety: behavior jailbreak, where an adversary induces an agent to execute dangerous OS-level operations with irreversible consequences. Existing benchmarks either evaluate safety at the semantic layer alone, missing physical-layer harms, or fail to isolate test cases, letting earlier runs contaminate later ones. We present LITMUS (LLM-agents In-OS Testing for Measuring Unsafe Subversion), a benchmark addressing both gaps via a semantic-physical dual verification mechanism and OS-level state rollback. LITMUS comprises 819 high-risk test cases organized into one harmful seed subset and six attack-extended subsets covering three adversarial paradigms (jailbreak speaking, skill injection, and entity wrapping), plus a fully automated multi-agent evaluation framework judging behavior at both conversational and OS-level physical layers. Evaluation across frontier agents reveals three findings: (1) current agents lack effective safety awareness, with strong models (e.g., Claude Sonnet 4.6) still executing 40.64% of high-risk operations; (2) agents exhibit pervasive Execution Hallucination (EH), verbally refusing a request while the dangerous operation has already completed at the system level, invisible to every prior semantic-only framework; and (3) skill injection and entity wrapping attacks achieve high success rates, exposing pronounced agent vulnerabilities. LITMUS provides the first standardized platform for reproducible, physically grounded behavioral safety evaluation of LLM agents in real OS environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 Oral. 32 pages, including appendices</p></details> |
| **[Zero-Shot Coordination among LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06409v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study zero-shot coordination (ZSC), where independently developed agents must coordinate at test time. While ZSC has been well studied in the RL literature, far less is known about the performance of LLM agents despite their increasing deployment in such settings. Existing work on LLM coordination often relies on specialised scaffolding that independently developed agents are unlikely to share in practice. Moreover, evaluations on complex environments (e.g., Hanabi) make it difficult to pinpoint the sources of coordination failure. In contrast, we focus on simple, general-purpose scaffolds in minimal environments designed to isolate specific coordination challenges. Our results show that even in these controlled settings, frontier LLM agents struggle to coordinate, largely due to limited understanding of the coordination problem and weak reasoning about their partner's beliefs. While LLMs introduce semantic information as an additional axis for coordination, they nonetheless fail to exploit this structure effectively. Towards this, we propose Coordination-friendly definitions (CFDs) as a principled approach for enabling robust coordination among LLM agents. Finally, we show that CFDs can be discovered automatically, removing the need for manual engineering. Code is available at https://github.com/ahayler/zsc-llm-agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 Poster. ICLR 26 MALGAI Workshop Oral. The first two authors contributed equally</p></details> |
| **[CLEAR: Structured Specifications for Controllable Long-Horizon ML Research Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06263v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly being used to automate machine learning (ML) research workflows, ranging from code development and experiment execution to research reproduction and scientific discovery. Recent systems demonstrate that LLMs can perform substantial portions of ML research engineering with limited human intervention. These advances suggest a shift from automating individual research tasks toward automating long-horizon workflows that transform research artifacts into executable ML systems. However, ML research engineering remains substantially more difficult than isolated code generation. A research artifact typically specifies a system only implicitly through a mixture of natural-language descriptions, equations, pseudo-code, implementation details, datasets, and evaluation protocols. An agent must first infer the intended computational structure, translate it into executable components, coordinate their dependencies, and repeatedly diagnose and repair failures during execution. Consequently, the quality of the final system depends not only on code generation, but also on whether the agent can maintain a consistent representation of what should be implemented throughout this long-horizon process.</p></details> | 11 pages, 4 figures |
| **[Analysis of Local Open-Weight LLM Agents on Vulnerability-Reproduction Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06257v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-weight language models that fit on a single consumer GPU are becoming capable on a growing range of tasks, and they matter because they can run locally without sending the code to a hosted service. Vulnerability reproduction, turning a vulnerability description into an input that crashes the vulnerable build, is a natural target for them, since it is an automated analysis task closer to test generation than to code comprehension and it runs against a build that must be executed rather than read. In order to assess whether current models can perform it, we evaluate twelve configurations of local models, spanning three generations, two architectures and three quantization levels, on ten CyberGym vulnerability-reproduction tasks, three runs each under one harness, and release the full artefact. We show that every configuration, down to the 4B model, solves most of the six easier tasks, and configurations separate only on the remaining four. For the harder tasks, the 27B model is required. Quantization did not change what the models solved in any matched pair, but it changed how fast they got there, and the mixture of experts reached the same scores in less wall clock. We also examine the agent trajectories. Almost every run we examined, including those that fail, names the vulnerable function and states the correct hypothesis, and what separates a solved task from an unsolved one is whether that hypothesis becomes a working input.</p></details> | <details><summary>35 pa...</summary><p>35 pages, 6 figures, 5 tables. Preprint of a manuscript under review at the Journal of Systems and Software (Elsevier). Artefact: https://doi.org/10.57967/hf/10888</p></details> |
| **[HarnessFlow: MCTS-Guided Harness Optimization for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06254v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The performance of large language model (LLM) agents depends on both the underlying model and the harness that governs interaction with tools and the environment. Yet harness design remains largely manual, and automating it requires navigating an open-ended search space through costly end-to-end evaluations. We introduce HarnessFlow, a framework for optimizing complete executable harnesses under a limited candidate-evaluation budget, with the model and task environment held fixed. HarnessFlow organizes candidates and their evaluation histories in a refinement tree, using a Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS)-based policy to balance exploitation and exploration. A coding agent refines selected harnesses using archived code, execution feedback, and a library of reusable harness mechanisms. With 15 refinement attempts per benchmark, HarnessFlow achieves the highest mean test scores among the compared methods on six benchmarks spanning agentic tasks, coding, and web interaction. The optimized harnesses achieve an average score of 69.48%, outperforming the initial harnesses and Meta-Harness by 10.89 and 3.95 percentage points, respectively, while incurring lower average per-task execution costs than hand-engineered baselines. These results suggest that structured harness search offers a promising route toward automating the design of capable and efficient LLM agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, 8 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[Harness the Memory: A Holistic Evaluation of Memory Substrates in Memory Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15008v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Memory is becoming core infrastructure for long-horizon LLM agents, yet existing evaluations offer limited guidance on which memory substrate, namely the underlying medium in which memory is represented and stored, should be used under different operating regimes. We introduce a unified suite of 26 performance and efficiency metrics, including auxiliary-LLM costs attributed to the write, read, and management stages, and apply it identically to 11 memory methods in a controlled harness that varies only the memory substrate. The methods cover dense and sparse indices, text records, structural stores, hierarchical stores, refinement-based memories, parametric updates, and activation-compatible context mechanisms, and we evaluate them with three backbone models on four benchmark suites spanning user-centric question answering and agent-centric decision-making. Our results show that no single substrate consistently dominates: broad retrieval benefits long-context factual QA, while excessive retrieval can harm sequential decision-making by shifting attention away from action-critical context. Scalability introduces a further routing axis, as substrates that perform well at moderate history lengths can become costly or brittle at longer horizons. These regime-dependent reversals make substrate selection a design axis. Choosing a substrate per observable operating regime improves over the best fixed substrate on our benchmarks, while query-level routing leaves most of the oracle headroom open. The findings provide empirical guidance for designing efficient, reliable, and regime-aware long-term memory for LLM agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026, Evaluations and Datasets Track</p></details> |
| **[EvolveNet: Collaborative Harness Evolution for Agent Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04968v3)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An LLM agent can acquire persistent improvements by evolving its harness---the executable program governing model calls, tool use, verification, and recovery---without updating model weights. When the same harness serves users with different workloads, isolated evolution limits the sharing of useful discoveries, while centralized adaptation can introduce conflicting task-specific changes. We introduce EvolveNet, a framework for collaborative harness evolution that connects local specialization with shared capability accumulation. Clients evolve the same broadcast harness on their local workloads in parallel, and a server composes their adaptations into an updated shared program. Redistribution makes accepted discoveries available to all clients, allowing subsequent local evolution to build on improvements accumulated across the collaboration. EvolveNet supports this process through evidence-guided, scope-typed program aggregation and behavioral validation. Our theoretical analysis provides sufficient conditions for retaining and combining complementary improvements despite program interactions. Experiments across five settings show improved shared-harness performance, with ablation and behavioral analyses supporting the contribution of composition beyond specialist selection alone.</p></details> | 29 pages, 3 figures |
| **[TTHE: Test-Time Harness Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08124v3)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The behavior of an LLM agent is determined not only by the underlying model, but also by its harness: the executable program that constructs context, invokes tools, verifies intermediate results, and recovers from failures. Existing approaches typically optimize harnesses on training or development data and freeze the resulting programs at test time, limiting adaptation to shifts in task distributions, failure modes, and tool interactions. We ask whether the harness can instead be optimized during evaluation using unlabeled execution traces. We introduce Test-Time Harness Evolution (TTHE), which treats the executable harness as the state of test-time adaptation. On each unlabeled test batch, TTHE iteratively evolves a population of candidate harnesses through agentic proposers that reason over execution traces and explore diverse edit directions. A judge selects a candidate using execution traces and execution-derived proxy signals. The selected harness determines the current batch's outputs and initializes adaptation on the next. Crucially, the solver, proposers, and judge operate through different harnesses around the same frozen LLM, without weight updates, access to final-answer labels, or a separately trained adaptation model. Across six execution-grounded benchmarks spanning text-to-SQL, competitive programming, software engineering, data-science coding, terminal-based tasks, and agentic tool use, TTHE improves fixed ReAct-style baseline harnesses through persistent, inspectable program adaptation. These results recast test-time adaptation for LLM agents as evolution over executable control programs and identify execution-derived proxy reliability as a central challenge for robust unsupervised agent improvement.</p></details> | 22 pages, 5 figures |
| **[SBVUL: Statement-Based Vulnerability Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06135v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many recent approaches for vulnerability detection use large language models (LLMs) to classify functions as vulnerable or benign, leaving developers to locate the exploitable statements. Furthermore, while these approaches have shown performance improvements, they still fall short in distinguishing vulnerable from patched real-world functions. This paper presents SBVul, an agentic approach that detects exploitable statements in C/C++ functions. In Phase~1, an LLM agent explores the function and its repository and identifies candidate statements. In Phase~2, a second agent analyzes the control and data flow of each candidate and confirms it, rules it out, or marks it uncertain. We created a manually verified ground truth of exploitable statements for 200 pairs of the PrimeVul Paired test set and ran SBVul with the open-weight models Qwen 3.8 and DeepSeek V4.1 Flash and Anthropic's Sonnet 5.5 and Opus 5.5. Phase~1 identifies ground-truth statements in 56--77% of the vulnerable functions, and Phase~2 confirms such a statement with an explanation accepted by an LLM judge in 26--73%. However, all models also confirm candidates in 23--61% of the patched functions. Across vulnerable and patched functions, the highest statement-level precision is 0.315 at a recall of 0.605 obtained with Opus 5.5 (xhigh). At the function level, SBVul with Opus 5.5 classifies 42.0% of the pairs correctly, compared with 33.8% reported for the best previous approach.</p></details> |  |
| **[When Disclosure Is Compulsory: Evaluating Ride-Hailing Rebalancing Policies with Self-Interested LLM Driver Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41132v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The EU Platform Work Directive, to be transposed by December 2026, requires ride-hailing platforms to state in plain language the main parameters of their automated decisions and how a worker's behaviour affects them. Such rules are exactly what a large language model (LLM) can read, so drivers will be able to let one decide for them. We compare two main families of rebalancing policy once their rules are public: a reactive policy (R) that sends idle drivers to cover requests just lost, and an anticipatory policy (A) that plans moves on a demand forecast. Each driver is an LLM agent maximizing its own net income, given no rules (N) or the rules at the directive's level of detail (L). We test two LLMs, Claude Haiku 5.5 and Jev, which understand the rules equally well, in a three-zone model and in Manhattan. With the rules disclosed, drivers follow the platform more closely: in Manhattan, the share of relocation offers they decline falls from about four in five to one in four under both policies. Yet the effects differ by policy. For the platform, disclosure costs R 3.7% of its profit in Manhattan, as R now pays for moves drivers used to make for free, while A's profit is unchanged; the two policies, which tie on profit under full compliance, end 6.1% apart. For drivers, disclosure raises income in the small model under both policies and LLMs; for users, it raises the service rate there by 10 to 18 percentage points. In Manhattan, self-interested drivers also make A a win-win-win against full compliance, raising driver income, platform profit and service rate by 10 to 12% each. Rebalancing policies that perform equally well under full compliance can thus show opposite results under mandatory disclosure. The study offers a framework to evaluate such policies under the new requirement and is, to our knowledge, the first to do so with LLM agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[RetryBench: Separating Retry Permission from Recovery Outcomes in GPT Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15884v4)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents that call external tools must decide what to do when a tool call times out: retrying risks duplicate side effects, while not retrying can leave the task incomplete. We present RetryBench, an evaluation protocol for retry decisions and failure recovery in tool-using LLM agents. Each decision is scored on retry permission (is the retry justified by an idempotency-key guarantee or proof of non-execution?), settled effects (duplicates and omissions), and completion, by replaying it across all hidden execution worlds consistent with the observation. On four synthetic software-engineering tasks, we run two controlled prompt-sensitivity studies (1,728 decisions). Study 1 (idempotency-key descriptions): with the contract fixed, GPT-5.6 Luna reuses the valid idempotency key in 10/40 decisions when an extra key is described as a "Candidate recovery key" but in 39/40 when it is an "Alternative key string"; across three further phrasings per family the gap is 27/120 versus 113/120. In contrast, human diagnosis converts failed traces into corrective knowledge and drives sustained improvement: a single round of human diagnosis outperforms four rounds of self-judgment by 7.0-10.9%. These findings point to a simple division of labor: let agents organize procedural memory, and invest human effort where it matters most -- diagnosis.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages (10 main + references and appendix), 6 figures, 14 tables. Peizhi Niu and Ge Shi contributed equally</p></details> |
| **[SLATE: A Large-Scale Benchmark for Long-Horizon API Selection in E-commerce Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03982v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprises increasingly want LLM agents to automate standard operating procedures over their software stacks -- mapping each step of a procedure to the right call among thousands of internal APIs, without hand-authored step-to-API mappings. The core difficulty is API selection at scale: choosing and composing the correct tools over a long horizon, judged only by whether the overall task succeeds. Current tool-use benchmarks do not test this regime -- they expose only a handful of tools, evaluate single-step invocation, or grade multi-step behavior with subjective LLM-as-a-judge scores that penalize valid alternative trajectories. We introduce SLATE (Synthetic Large-scale API Toolkit for E-commerce), a large-scale benchmark that tests large-toolset, long-horizon API selection in a realistic enterprise domain. SLATE couples (i) queries decomposed into hierarchical, tool-grounded procedures over a ~1,000-tool e-commerce library, (ii) a deterministic, context-aware simulator that returns recorded outputs for matching calls and default responses otherwise, and (iii) an end-to-end task-success metric -- a task succeeds when it reaches every reference outcome -- that credits diverse-but-valid trajectories while remaining fully automatic and reproducible. Experiments with six open decision models and the language models they are built on, from 3B to 12B parameters, show that even the best model completes only 5.0% of the tasks, establishing SLATE as a challenging testbed for reliable enterprise tool use. We release SLATE, its simulator, and the evaluation harness at https://geshijoker.github.io/slate/</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 13 figures, 6 tables. Camera-ready version for the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Agentic AI Benchmarks and Applications for Enterprise Tasks (AABA4ET). Project page: https://geshijoker.github.io/slate/</p></details> |
| **[Measuring Model-Specific Alignment Drift in LLM-Based Opinion Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03980v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly used as synthetic participants in opinion-dynamics experiments, often assuming that an agent's behaviour is fully determined by its prompt. Across six LLMs, committed personas are intrinsically stable: in each of the five models tested without peer input, every agent keeps its assigned side for 20 rounds. Under selective attention, one LLM maintains separated opinion clusters under stance-homophilous attention. Qualitative profiles persist at doubled population and horizon and on a second issue. Such heterogeneity makes the LLM choice a first-class validity variable for social simulation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Labtune: Treating Lab Organization and Information Sharing as Experimental Variables in LLM-Agent Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03935v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>De-siloing can create groupthink; silos can prevent knowledge sharing and reduce research efficiency. I propose treating laboratory structure and information sharing as experimental variables. I introduce Labtune, an open framework for bounded research workflows with user-selected laboratory organization, communication policy, model configuration and budget. Users can choose among six editable designs, from multidisciplinary silos and transdisciplinary teams to co-leaders, hub-and-spoke structures, flat co-labs and a single scientist. Information sharing can independently vary from none to high detail, with an optional channel for conditional abstractions called wisdom. Sharing is enforced by the controller at phase barriers rather than through unrestricted agent chat, while delegation does not create additional budget or permissions. Development evidence is separated from protected confirmation, and an optional multi-lab layer allows laboratories with different structures to exchange filtered information. Labtune is an idea and system-description paper, not a benchmark result. Its contribution is to make laboratory organization and information sharing configurable and measurable under matched budgets. I do not claim that one design or level of sharing is best; the aim is to create a concrete experimental space in which those differences can be tested.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages. Exploratory preprint</p></details> |
| **[FedReady: Bounded Agents for Federation Formation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03586v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated learning (FL) runtimes standardize aggregation and deployment, but each new study still requires federation formation: selecting task-eligible sites and preparing their local data and task code for federated training or analysis. We present FedReady, a system that performs federation formation with bounded LLM agents. Agents propose shared contracts, local adapters, and task code; the runtime owns workflow state and accepts artifacts only after checks tied to their digests. Candidate site discovery prioritizes recall; local eligibility verification checks task support before site admission. Once participating sites and artifacts are fixed, conventional federated training or analysis proceeds without readiness agents. Across 39 simulated retinal sites and four tasks, discovery reaches recall 1.00 at precision 0.65; local eligibility verification removes all 17 false positives without losing an eligible site-task pair. The participating sites supply 45,575 task-record instances and complete four 100-round jobs, including one with a runtime-generated contract. Across ten patient cohorts, preparation supports seven patient populations and holds endpoints at three sources as unsupported. Comparison with manually prepared reference populations and analysis (the manual reference) gives a maximum survival-curve difference of 0.001133. We also analyze 19 development failures, targeted classification ablations, and standalone and heterogeneous agent configurations. | **[Can LLM Agents Be Forward-Deployed Engineers? Iterative feedback lowered median rollout error for eight of ten models. | **[Ask, Don't Equip: Question-Driven Coding Agents for Deep Kernel Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03517v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents for GPU kernel optimization sit at two ends of a structure-freedom tradeoff. Open-ended agents let a self-directed model mutate a population of kernels and pay for the freedom in trials: thousands of evaluations, repeated edits, and wrong candidates. Both respond to hard targets by equipping the agent with more: parsed profiler output, disassembly analysis, retrieval over documentation and libraries, and stores of lessons. We argue that both lack direction, a hypothesis about where the remaining gap is, and that a short question asked at the right moment supplies it without narrowing what the agent may do next. We present KernelSeek, a question-driven agent that makes an existing CUDA kernel faster with no operator-specific knowledge and no faster implementation to imitate: a general-purpose coding agent, a minimal harness that enforces a verifiable goal, and questions at decision points, first from a human, then from a bank the agent applies to itself. On the same task, evaluator, hardware, and week, a tool-rich autonomous agent built in the prevailing style plateaued at 0.50x FlashAttention-3 after 133 model-driven steps; KernelSeek reached 1.13x. It then produced dependency-free FlashAttention forward kernels for Hopper and Blackwell, single CUDA files with no CUTLASS, CuTe, cuBLAS, or cuDNN, correct on all 182 scenarios per architecture and 1.16x FlashAttention-3 and 1.46x FlashAttention-4 (geometric mean). | **[Optimize the Harness, Not the Model: Fitting a Minimal Agent to a Self-Hostable LLM for Investigative Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03434v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Government intelligence teams need LLM agents that can analyze large, messy evidence collections, but security and cost constraints often rule out hosted frontier models and broad, hard-to-audit tool surfaces. | **[IdeaTaste: Do LLMs Share the Research Taste of Human Researchers?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03424v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Research taste, the judgment of what to read, which problems to pursue, and which methods to try, increasingly shapes the work of AI scientists, yet it remains far less understood than their ability to execute research. We introduce IdeaTaste, a benchmark that scores an LLM by how closely the research proposals it writes from a literature snapshot frozen at 2025 match the work the same communities published in 2026. Our experiments span six contemporary language models and four research areas from established to emerging fields, covering more than 90{,}000 proposals in total. LLM agents fall well short of human communities in both the literature they cite and the ideas they state, reaching about 26\% of the human level on citation matching and only 18\% on idea matching. Their populations can cite as diversely as a community and take up its tasks and methods, yet rarely arrive at the concrete approaches and research plans it pursues. Neither richer field context nor larger populations substantially close this gap. In blinded expert review, AI proposals trail the 2026 papers least in presentation and most in expected success, echoing their gap in concrete approaches, and those whose ideas no human paper shares fare worst, suggesting weaker directions rather than overlooked opportunities. These results indicate that the main bottleneck in AI research taste lies less in finding the directions a community works on than in turning them into the concrete approaches it goes on to pursue. We release IdeaTaste as an open, continuously updated leaderboard (https://cxcscmu.github.io/IdeaTaste) that benchmarks contemporary AI proposals against future publication cycles.</p></details> |  |

To address this, we propose DARE (Distribution-Aware Retrieval Embedding), a representation learning framework incorporating these data characteristics into R function retrieval. Our contributions are fourfold: (i) RPKB, a curated knowledge base of 8,191 high-quality R functions; (ii) DARE, the distribution-aware retrieval framework; (iii) RCodingAgent, an R-oriented agent integrating DARE for end-to-end analysis; and (iv) RStatBench, a comprehensive benchmark of 100 statistical tasks. | **[SQLoom: Set-Contrastive Learning of Syntactic, Semantic, and Logical Dependencies for Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03298v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text-to-SQL translates natural language questions into SQL queries, enabling database access without writing SQL. Fine-tuning open-weight large language models (LLMs) is cost-effective, but existing methods treat SQL as ordinary text and optimize token-level likelihood without explicitly modeling its syntactic, semantic, and logical requirements. LLM agents propose this supervision, which a SQL parser and execution on counterexample databases verify. | **[Do Agents Still Follow the Rules? Its tool-calling agent loop lets us measure rule compliance from actions taken during execution, bringing the evaluation closer to deployed agent workflows. Using ARCBench, we examine where violations after compaction come from, and how different compaction configurations and adversarial settings change their sources. We find that compaction rarely deletes the rule: most violations after compaction occur while the rule is still in the compacted history, with the agent treating a later request as permission, ignoring the rule, and proceeding anyway. Where violations come from depends on the configuration: how the rule is delivered decides whether the agent would break it even without compaction, while the compaction strategy and budget decide whether the rule survives. The strongest compactor-targeted attack more than doubles violations by corrupting the rule in the summary and changing how the agents behave, whereas the strongest defense restores the rule in most summary but still violates. Finally, retaining a rule does not guarantee compliance: compacted runs that retain the rule violate more than twice as often as runs with the full history.</p></details> |  |

On the agent side, the number of mounted tools is limited by the LLM context window and inference overhead; mounting a large tool set increases token usage and inference latency and can reduce task success rate. Code available at https://github.com/Tdadada/BLPO</p></details> |

In this work, we systematically diagnose the prediction-to-action pipeline along three dimensions: how world-model predictions are integrated into decision making, whether the predictions themselves are reliable, and whether their content is faithfully used. Across seven policy models and two safety environments, we find that stronger prediction involvement does not yield monotonic safety gains and can reduce task completion while substantially increasing inference cost. We further find that world-model predictions remain insufficiently reliable, especially over longer horizons, where safety prediction degrades substantially relative to near-term forecasting. Finally, we find that safety gains can persist even under corrupted predictions, while locally verified predictions still leave subsequent safety violations, showing that predictive content is not faithfully incorporated into the decision process. Together, these findings expose a fundamental prediction-to-action gap in current predictive guardrails: producing more or seemingly accurate foresight is insufficient unless that foresight is both reliable and faithfully translated into safer decisions.</p></details> |  |

Existing HLS optimization approaches, including both heuristic-based methods and recent LLM-assisted pipelines, typically operate at fixed abstraction levels or in weakly coupled stages, limiting their ability to leverage the strong interplay between source code restructuring and directive search outcomes. Our key insight is that HLS optimization can be reframed from a fixed, hardwired transformation pipeline into a skill-based agentic control problem: an LLM agent equipped with modular, executable optimization skills and a feedback-driven orchestrator can autonomously navigate the coupled space of code restructuring and directive search. On real-world HLS benchmarks, HLSClaw achieves a 39.12x geometric-mean latency speedup, outperforming the compiler baseline ScaleHLS by 1.98x, and surpassing the commercial coding agents Claude Code and Codex by 1.86x and 1.70x, respectively, while maintaining strong timing robustness.</p></details> |  |
| **[ATrust: Adaptive Trust Boundary Expansion for Securing LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02741v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language model (LLM) agents take on more complex tasks with greater autonomy, security has become a major obstacle to their broader deployment. To address this problem, we propose ATrust, a defense mechanism that adaptively expands the trust boundary during agent runtime. Starting from the user query as the root trust anchor, ATrust evaluates external observations against the current trust boundary and execution state, progressively admitting task-essential information as new trust anchors. This preserves necessary environmental feedback while excluding irrelevant content, whether benign or malicious, from subsequent decision-making, thereby limiting unnecessary exposure to external environment. We evaluate ATrust on AgentDojo and AgentDyn, where it achieves the strongest overall security--utility trade-off among existing defenses. With GPT-4o as the engine model, ATrust reduces the attack success rate on AgentDyn from 37.80% to 4.37% while enhancing utility by 10.00%, outperforming AuthGraph, the state-of-the-art defense, by 12.33%. Experimental results show that IssueLighter consistently and substantially outperforms all baseline methods, with an average F1 improvement of over 20 percentage points. Experimental results demonstrate consistent gains in task performance and rollout efficiency across model scales, highlighting structured hindsight credit assignment as an effective approach to long-horizon agent learning.</p></details> | 19 pages, 4 figures |

METHODS: A multivocal literature review covered 36 practitioner systems and 51 academic papers; we consolidated practices into patterns and derived the reference architecture from patterns, source evidence, and architectural reasoning. | **[Does the Judge See What the Evaluation Requires? ## Medical Large Language Models
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[A Self-Evolving Agent for Longitudinal Personal Health Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13940v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models and agents show strong single-encounter question answering and evidence interpretation but struggle to accumulate and govern experience across encounters. To filter out erroneous lessons before they affect future behaviour, we self-train JevGate, a lightweight System-One model that decides which lessons enter memory; each decision takes a median of 86 milliseconds and about 1,000 input tokens, sustaining experience accumulation at negligible run-time overhead. On a longitudinal benchmark of 90-day simulated daily interactions for 20 users built from real MIMIC-IV trajectories, HealthJev outperformed a memory-free ReAct agent and three memory-augmented frameworks, with all paired comparisons significant. Across six tasks spanning clinical records, medical imaging, genomics and clinical text, HealthJev improved every task with a mean gain of 13.3 percentage points over the base condition, surpassing all baselines. The frozen library transferred to six further backbones with macro-average gains of 6.8 to 15.1 percentage points, and one task's library brought gains on other tasks. These results indicate that governed, self-evolving memory supports longitudinal personal health management beyond single encounters. HealthJev is publicly available at https://github.com/HC-Guo/Self-Evolving_HealthJev.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 4 figures. We propose PFedQFT, a quantum-inspired framework for personalized federated fine-tuning with a compact shared adapter. Specifically, PFedQFT constructs reusable orthonormal adaptation bases from compact skew-symmetric generators using a truncated matrix-exponential approximation followed by QR orthonormalization. Compatible projections reuse these bases, while a globally shared full core preserves the flexibility of each projection. Each client further maintains a lightweight private residual operator, whose directions are learned globally and whose coefficients remain local. Experiments on seven public medical tasks and multiple LLM backbones show substantial reductions in trainable parameters and per-round adapter upload volume, with competitive predictive performance on most tasks.</p></details> | 10 pages, 5 figures |

Our findings reveal limitations in standard medical benchmarks and underscore that scaling alone does not resolve LLM reliability issues.</p></details> |  |
| **[Scaling Recurrence-aware Foundation Models for Clinical Records via Next-Visit Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.24562v2)** | 2026-04-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While large-scale pretraining has revolutionized language modeling, its potential remains underexplored in healthcare with structured electronic health records (EHRs). We present RAVEN, a novel generative pretraining strategy for sequential EHR data based on Recurrence-Aware next-Visit EveNt prediction. Leveraging a dataset of over one million unique individuals, our model learns to autoregressively generate tokenized clinical events for the next visit conditioned on patient history. We introduce regularization on predicting repeated events and highlight a key pitfall in EHR-based foundation model evaluations: repeated event tokens can inflate performance metrics when new onsets are not distinguished from subsequent occurrences. Furthermore, we empirically investigate the scaling behaviors in a data-constrained, compute-saturated regime, showing that simply increasing model size is suboptimal without commensurate increases in data volume. We evaluate our model via zero-shot prediction for forecasting the incidence of a diverse set of diseases, where it rivals fully fine-tuned representation-based Transformer models and outperforms both standard simulation-based next-token approaches and a prompted medical large language model baseline. Finally, without additional parameter updates, we show that RAVEN can generalize to an external patient cohort under lossy clinical code mappings and feature coverage gaps.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Multi-Domain Red Teaming Framework for Safety, Robustness, and Fairness Evaluation of Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00027v1)** | 2026-04-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed across healthcare, yet existing benchmarks fail to capture model behavior under adversarial or ethically complex conditions common in clinical practice. We developed a multi-domain red teaming framework evaluating eleven contemporary LLMs across 690 clinically grounded scenarios spanning nine domains and over 150 subcategories. Scenarios incorporated adversarial transformations, and responses were assessed using a seven-dimension rubric with LLM-assisted scoring and human-in-the-loop validation. Results revealed substantial performance variance, with mean scores ranging from 0.791 to 0.984. Critically, several high-performing systems produced complete failures in individual safety-critical scenarios, demonstrating that aggregate accuracy masks clinically meaningful risk. The highest-performing systems (X-BAI, GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.1) achieved scores above 0.97 with low variance, while performance varied significantly across domains. Equity-related tasks showed 10-20% error amplification with demographic modifications, and human reviewers identified clinically relevant failures missed by automated evaluation. Our findings demonstrate that performance variance and worst-case failures provide more clinically meaningful reliability indicators than mean accuracy alone, and that hybrid evaluation approaches combining automation with clinician oversight are essential for credible safety assessment.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 4 figures. To be presented at the Text2Story 2026 Workshop (Delft, The Netherlands, 29 March 2026); CEUR Workshop Proceedings (forthcoming). Affiliation: John Snow Labs Inc</p></details> |
| **[From Retinal Evidence to Safe Decisions: RETINA-SAFE and ECRT for Hallucination Risk Triage in Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.05348v1)** | 2026-04-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hallucinations in medical large language models (LLMs) remain a safety-critical issue, particularly when available evidence is insufficient or conflicting. We study this problem in diabetic retinopathy (DR) decision settings and introduce RETINA-SAFE, an evidence-grounded benchmark aligned with retinal grading records, comprising 12,522 samples. RETINA-SAFE is organized into three evidence-relation tasks: E-Align (evidence-consistent), E-Conflict (evidence-conflicting), and E-Gap (evidence-insufficient). We further propose ECRT (Evidence-Conditioned Risk Triage), a two-stage white-box detection framework: Stage 1 performs Safe/Unsafe risk triage, and Stage 2 refines unsafe cases into contradiction-driven versus evidence-gap risks. ECRT leverages internal representation and logit shifts under CTX/NOCTX conditions, with class-balanced training for robust learning. Under evidence-grouped (not patient-disjoint) splits across multiple backbones, ECRT provides strong Stage-1 risk triage and explicit subtype attribution, improves Stage-1 balanced accuracy by +0.15 to +0.19 over external uncertainty and self-consistency baselines and by +0.02 to +0.07 over the strongest adapted supervised baseline, and consistently exceeds a single-stage white-box ablation on Stage-1 balanced accuracy. These findings support white-box internal signals grounded in retinal evidence as a practical route to interpretable medical LLM risk triage.</p></details> |  |
| **[MAPLE: Elevating Medical Reasoning from Statistical Consensus to Process-Led Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08987v1)** | 2026-03-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in medical large language models have explored Test-Time Reinforcement Learning (TTRL) to enhance reasoning. However, standard TTRL often relies on majority voting (MV) as a heuristic supervision signal, which can be unreliable in complex medical scenarios where the most frequent reasoning path is not necessarily the clinically correct one. In this work, we propose a novel and unified training paradigm that integrates medical process reward models with TTRL to bridge the gap between test-time scaling (TTS) and parametric model optimization. Specifically, we advance the TTRL framework by replacing the conventional MV with a fine-grained, expert-aligned supervision paradigm using Med-RPM. This integration ensures that reinforcement learning is guided by medical correctness rather than mere consensus, effectively distilling search-based intelligence into the model's parametric memory. Extensive evaluations on four different benchmarks have demonstrated that our developed method consistently and significantly outperforms current TTRL and standalone PRM selection. Our findings establish that transitioning from stochastic heuristics to structured, step-wise rewards is essential for developing reliable and scalable medical AI systems</p></details> |  |
| **[Silent Sabotage During Fine-Tuning: Few-Shot Rationale Poisoning of Compact Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02262v1)** | 2026-02-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is essential for the development of medical large language models (LLMs), yet prior poisoning studies have mainly focused on the detectable backdoor attacks. We propose a novel poisoning attack targeting the reasoning process of medical LLMs during SFT. Unlike backdoor attacks, our method injects poisoned rationales into few-shot training data, leading to stealthy degradation of model performance on targeted medical topics. Results showed that knowledge overwriting was ineffective, while rationale poisoning caused significant decline on the accuracy of the target subject, as long as no correct samples of the same subject appear in the dataset. A minimum number and ratio of poisoned samples was needed to carry out an effective and stealthy attack, which was more efficient and accurate than catastrophic forgetting. We demonstrate though this study the risk of SFT-stage poisoning, hoping to spur more studies of defense in the sensitive medical domain.</p></details> |  |
| **[DeVisE: Behavioral Testing of Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15339v3)** | 2026-02-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied in clinical decision support, yet current evaluations rarely reveal whether their outputs reflect genuine medical reasoning or superficial correlations. We introduce DeVisE (Demographics and Vital signs Evaluation), a behavioral testing framework that probes fine-grained clinical understanding through controlled counterfactuals. Using intensive care unit (ICU) discharge notes from MIMIC-IV, we construct both raw (real-world) and template-based (synthetic) variants with single-variable perturbations in demographic (age, gender, ethnicity) and vital sign attributes. We evaluate eight LLMs, spanning general-purpose and medical variants, under zero-shot setting. Model behavior is analyzed through (1) input-level sensitivity, capturing how counterfactuals alter perplexity, and (2) downstream reasoning, measuring their effect on predicted ICU length-of-stay and mortality. Overall, our results show that standard task metrics obscure clinically relevant differences in model behavior, with models differing substantially in how consistently and proportionally they adjust predictions to counterfactual perturbations.</p></details> | <details><summary>Camer...</summary><p>Camera-ready version published at Findings of the EACL 2026</p></details> |
| **[PeruMedQA: Benchmarking Large Language Models (LLMs) on Peruvian Medical Exams -- Dataset Construction and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.11517v2)** | 2026-02-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>BACKGROUND: Medical large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance in answering medical examinations. However, the extent to which this high performance is transferable to medical questions in Spanish and from a Latin American country remains unexplored. This knowledge is crucial as LLM-based medical applications gain traction in Latin America. AIMS: To build a dataset of questions medical examinations taken by Peruvian physicians pursuing specialty training; to fine-tune a LLM on this dataset; to evaluate and compare the performance in terms of accuracy between vanilla LLMs and the fine-tuned LLM. METHODS: We curated PeruMedQA, a multiple-choice question-answering (MCQA) dataset containing 8,380 questions spanning 12 specialties (2018-2025). We selected ten medical LLMs, including medgemma-4b-it and medgemma-27b-text-it, and developed zero-shot task specific prompts to answer the questions. We employed parameter-efficient fine tuning (PEFT) and low-rand adaptation (LoRA) to fine-tune medgemma-4b-it utilizing all questions except those from 2025 (test set). RESULTS: Medgemma-27b showed the highest accuracy across all specialities, achieving the highest score of 89.29% in Psychiatry; yet, in two specialties, OctoMed-7B exhibited slight superiority: Neurosurgery with 77.27% and 77.38, respectively; and Radiology with 76.13% and 77.39%, respectively. Across specialties, most LLMs with <10 billion parameters exhibited <50% of correct answers. The fine-tuned version of medgemma-4b-it emerged victorious against all LLMs with <10 billion parameters and rivaled a LLM with 70 billion parameters across various examinations. CONCLUSIONS: For medical AI applications and research that require knowledge bases from Spanish-speaking countries and those exhibiting similar epidemiological profile to Peru's, interested parties should utilize medgemma-27b-text-it.</p></details> | <details><summary>https...</summary><p>https://github.com/rodrigo-carrillo/PeruMedQA</p></details> |
| **[MedDialogRubrics: A Comprehensive Benchmark and Evaluation Framework for Multi-turn Medical Consultations in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.03023v2)** | 2026-01-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical conversational AI (AI) plays a pivotal role in the development of safer and more effective medical dialogue systems. However, existing benchmarks and evaluation frameworks for assessing the information-gathering and diagnostic reasoning abilities of medical large language models (LLMs) have not been rigorously evaluated. To address these gaps, we present MedDialogRubrics, a novel benchmark comprising 5,200 synthetically constructed patient cases and over 60,000 fine-grained evaluation rubrics generated by LLMs and subsequently refined by clinical experts, specifically designed to assess the multi-turn diagnostic capabilities of LLM. Our framework employs a multi-agent system to synthesize realistic patient records and chief complaints from underlying disease knowledge without accessing real-world electronic health records, thereby mitigating privacy and data-governance concerns. We design a robust Patient Agent that is limited to a set of atomic medical facts and augmented with a dynamic guidance mechanism that continuously detects and corrects hallucinations throughout the dialogue, ensuring internal coherence and clinical plausibility of the simulated cases. Furthermore, we propose a structured LLM-based and expert-annotated rubric-generation pipeline that retrieves Evidence-Based Medicine (EBM) guidelines and utilizes the reject sampling to derive a prioritized set of rubric items ("must-ask" items) for each case. We perform a comprehensive evaluation of state-of-the-art models and demonstrate that, across multiple assessment dimensions, current models face substantial challenges. Our results indicate that improving medical dialogue will require advances in dialogue management architectures, not just incremental tuning of the base-model.</p></details> |  |
| **[Measuring Stability Beyond Accuracy in Small Open-Source Medical Large Language Models for Pediatric Endocrinology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.11567v1)** | 2025-12-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Small open-source medical large language models (LLMs) offer promising opportunities for low-resource deployment and broader accessibility. However, their evaluation is often limited to accuracy on medical multiple choice question (MCQ) benchmarks, and lacks evaluation of consistency, robustness, or reasoning behavior. We use MCQ coupled to human evaluation and clinical review to assess six small open-source medical LLMs (HuatuoGPT-o1 (Chen 2024), Diabetica-7B, Diabetica-o1 (Wei 2024), Meditron3-8B (Sallinen2025), MedFound-7B (Liu 2025), and ClinicaGPT-base-zh (Wang 2023)) in pediatric endocrinology. In deterministic settings, we examine the effect of prompt variation on models' output and self-assessment bias. In stochastic settings, we evaluate output variability and investigate the relationship between consistency and correctness. HuatuoGPT-o1-8B achieved the highest performance. The results show that high consistency across the model response is not an indicator of correctness, although HuatuoGPT-o1-8B showed the highest consistency rate. When tasked with selecting correct reasoning, both HuatuoGPT-o1-8B and Diabetica-o1 exhibit self-assessment bias and dependency on the order of the candidate explanations. Expert review of incorrect reasoning rationales identified a mix of clinically acceptable responses and clinical oversight. We further show that system-level perturbations, such as differences in CUDA builds, can yield statistically significant shifts in model output despite stable accuracy. This work demonstrates that small, semantically negligible prompt perturbations lead to divergent outputs, raising concerns about reproducibility of LLM-based evaluations and highlights the output variability under different stochastic regimes, emphasizing the need of a broader diagnostic framework to understand potential pitfalls in real-world clinical decision support scenarios.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 11 figures, accepted at 47 workshop Reproducible Artificial Intelligence (AAAI 2026, Singapore, January 27, 2026)</p></details> |
| **[Black-Box Behavioral Distillation Breaks Safety Alignment in Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.09403v1)** | 2025-12-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As medical large language models (LLMs) become increasingly integrated into clinical workflows, concerns around alignment robustness, and safety are escalating. Prior work on model extraction has focused on classification models or memorization leakage, leaving the vulnerability of safety-aligned generative medical LLMs underexplored. We present a black-box distillation attack that replicates the domain-specific reasoning of safety-aligned medical LLMs using only output-level access. By issuing 48,000 instruction queries to Meditron-7B and collecting 25,000 benign instruction response pairs, we fine-tune a LLaMA3 8B surrogate via parameter efficient LoRA under a zero-alignment supervision setting, requiring no access to model weights, safety filters, or training data. With a cost of $12, the surrogate achieves strong fidelity on benign inputs while producing unsafe completions for 86% of adversarial prompts, far exceeding both Meditron-7B (66%) and the untuned base model (46%). This reveals a pronounced functional-ethical gap, task utility transfers, while alignment collapses. To analyze this collapse, we develop a dynamic adversarial evaluation framework combining Generative Query (GQ)-based harmful prompt generation, verifier filtering, category-wise failure analysis, and adaptive Random Search (RS) jailbreak attacks. We also propose a layered defense system, as a prototype detector for real-time alignment drift in black-box deployments. Our findings show that benign-only black-box distillation exposes a practical and under-recognized threat: adversaries can cheaply replicate medical LLM capabilities while stripping safety mechanisms, underscoring the need for extraction-aware safety monitoring.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Practical Framework for Evaluating Medical AI Security: Reproducible Assessment of Jailbreaking and Privacy Vulnerabilities Across Clinical Specialties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08185v1)** | 2025-12-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed for clinical decision support across diverse specialties, yet systematic evaluation of their robustness to adversarial misuse and privacy leakage remains inaccessible to most researchers. Existing security benchmarks require GPU clusters, commercial API access, or protected health data -- barriers that limit community participation in this critical research area. We propose a practical, fully reproducible framework for evaluating medical AI security under realistic resource constraints. Our framework design covers multiple medical specialties stratified by clinical risk -- from high-risk domains such as emergency medicine and psychiatry to general practice -- addressing jailbreaking attacks (role-playing, authority impersonation, multi-turn manipulation) and privacy extraction attacks. All evaluation utilizes synthetic patient records requiring no IRB approval. The framework is designed to run entirely on consumer CPU hardware using freely available models, eliminating cost barriers. We present the framework specification including threat models, data generation methodology, evaluation protocols, and scoring rubrics. This proposal establishes a foundation for comparative security assessment of medical-specialist models and defense mechanisms, advancing the broader goal of ensuring safe and trustworthy medical AI systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>6 pag...</summary><p>6 pages, 1 figure, framework proposal</p></details> |
| **[OctoMed: Data Recipes for State-of-the-Art Multimodal Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.23269v1)** | 2025-11-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High-quality and carefully curated data is a cornerstone of training medical large language models, as it directly impacts both generalization and robustness to unseen clinical tasks. We investigate strategies for training and data curation to develop a robust multimodal reasoning model in the medical domain. Our work focuses on supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and explores data recipes that leverage structured reasoning traces. Using our proposed data recipe, we scale experiments to a dataset of over 8 million examples and 6.8 billion response tokens, achieving state-of-the-art performance among open-source models across diverse out-of-distribution medical benchmark tasks. Our results further indicate that curating a high-quality, diverse training dataset with varying structured reasoning trace lengths enables the fine-tuned model to self-calibrate its reasoning trajectory lengths based on the downstream task, without explicit supervision. We present key insights, describe the data curation strategy, and outline next steps toward developing robust medical vision-language reasoning system.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Alignment Paradox of Medical Large Language Models in Infertility Care: Decoupling Algorithmic Improvement from Clinical Decision-making Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18084v1)** | 2025-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly adopted in clinical decision support, yet aligning them with the multifaceted reasoning pathways of real-world medicine remains a major challenge. Using more than 8,000 infertility treatment records, we systematically evaluate four alignment strategies: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), and In-Context Learning (ICL) through a dual-layer framework combining automatic benchmarks with blinded doctor-in-the-loop assessments. GRPO achieves the highest algorithmic accuracy across multiple decision layers, confirming the value of reinforcement-based optimization for structured prediction tasks. However, clinicians consistently prefer the SFT model, citing clearer reasoning processes (p = 0.035) and higher therapeutic feasibility (p = 0.019). In blinded pairwise comparisons, SFT attains the highest winning rate (51.2%), outperforming both GRPO (26.2%) and even physicians' original decisions (22.7%). These results reveal an alignment paradox: algorithmic improvements do not necessarily translate into higher clinical trust, and may diverge from human-centered preferences. Our findings highlight the need for alignment strategies that prioritize clinically interpretable and practically feasible reasoning, rather than solely optimizing decision-level accuracy.</p></details> | 22 pages 5 figures |
